People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Well, I’m here because I’ve been trying to get someone to actually listen to what’s been going on at work. It’s been over a year now with this new ticket rotation and the security audits, and I know management keeps saying it’s just policy, they even show me logs like that proves something, but it feels like they’re building a paper trail against me.

I work in computer support, so I know how logs can be used, and I’ve seen enough “routine” procedures turn into blame later. Anyway, it’s not just a scheduling thing to me. I came because nobody there is taking it seriously.

I came because nobody there is taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is intentionally trying to harm you or your career?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think there’s something intentional about it. I mean, they keep saying it’s just the new ticket rotation and the security audits, and they’ll pull up logs like that settles the whole matter, but logs only show what someone wants to track, you know? I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know how that stuff can be framed later.\n\nIt’s been going on for over a year, and it feels like they’re building a paper trail against me. Not “harm” like physically, but my career, yes. My reputation there. And once that starts, it’s hard to undo.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others outside of your workplace, such as friends or family, believe that management is intentionally trying to harm your career?\n\nPatient: My family mostly thinks I’m overreading it, I think. They haven’t seen the ticket rotation or the audit stuff the way I have. I try to explain that for over a year now this “policy” has been lining things up to make me look bad, like a paper trail, and management just keeps pointing to logs as if logs can’t be used that way.\n\nI mean, my brother doesn’t really understand computers beyond email, so when I say “logs,” he thinks it’s some neutral thing. But at work, in support, you know how these things can be framed. So no, I don’t think they fully believe me, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about management's intentions are based on facts that anyone could verify, or is it more of a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: I mean, there are facts there, yes. It’s not just a feeling. The ticket rotation changed, the security audits started happening more, and this has been going on for over a year. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but logs don’t prove intent, you know?\n\nThat’s the part people don’t get. In computer support, I’ve seen how documentation can be used later to make someone look like the problem. So yes, some of it is verifiable — the rotation, the audits, the logs — but what I’m saying is those things are being used to build a paper trail against me. And they act like I’m being unreasonable for noticing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ve considered it because people keep asking me that, yes. I’m not trying to be stubborn. But I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.

Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that explains everything. But in computer support, logs don’t tell the whole story. I’ve seen situations where something looks routine on paper, and then later it gets used to say, “See, this person is the problem.” It’s like when my old supervisor years ago used to say documentation is only as neutral as the person writing it—well, not exactly those words, but that was the idea.

So, I mean, could it theoretically not be about me? Maybe. But after a year of it lining up the way it has, it doesn’t feel random. It feels directed.

I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.

Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s policy,” like that settles it. But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and policies can be applied in a way that looks neutral but isn’t. I remember at another job, not the same thing exactly, but there were these reports people acted like were objective, and later they became ammunition. So when I see the pattern now, I’m not inventing it. I’m noticing what’s there.

I’ve had moments where I tried to doubt it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit there and think, okay, maybe the ticket rotation really is just some new system they rolled out, maybe the security audits are just policy like they keep saying. I’m in computer support, so I know how these records can look neutral while still being used to build a case. So the doubt doesn’t really last long. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.

But in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.

And I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.

Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.

But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.

Similar before? Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.

But this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.

So… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.

Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. In computer support, everything gets documented—tickets, timestamps, escalations—and people act like that makes it neutral. But if you’ve been around long enough, you know documentation can be shaped.

What I’m trying to say is, no, I don’t think I had another job where I felt it this clearly. This current place is the one where, for over a year, I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.

I mean, I haven’t noticed, like, someone following me around or anything like that. Mostly what I pay attention to is the workplace stuff, because that’s where the logs and tickets and all that are.\n\nAlthough, you know, these days everything is connected anyway — phones, email, badge systems, all these accounts — but I don’t want to make it sound like I’m saying my family or neighbors are watching me. That’s not really what I’ve been trying to explain. My concern is more with management and what they’re documenting at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the belief that management is building a paper trail against you is something that might be happening only in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know why you’re asking that, but no, I don’t think it’s just in my mind. That’s the frustrating part. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s policy,” like that settles it. But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and policies can be applied in a way that looks neutral but isn’t. I remember at another job, not the same thing exactly, but there were these reports people acted like were objective, and later they became ammunition. So when I see the pattern now, I’m not inventing it. I’m noticing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you doubted your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve had moments where I tried to doubt it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit there and think, okay, maybe the ticket rotation really is just some new system they rolled out, maybe the security audits are just policy like they keep saying. They show me logs and reports and act like that should end the discussion.\n\nBut then I look at how it’s been going on for over a year, and how the same things keep getting documented around me, and it’s hard to believe it’s just routine. I’m in computer support, so I know how these records can look neutral while still being used to build a case. So the doubt doesn’t really last long. It keeps coming back to feeling like they’re making a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your interpretation of the situation at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.\n\nBut in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.\n\nAnd I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nBut I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.\n\nBut this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.\n\nSo… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. But at work, in support, you know how these things can be framed. So no, I don’t think they fully believe me, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about management's intentions are based on facts that anyone could verify, or is it more of a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: I mean, there are facts there, yes. It’s not just a feeling. The ticket rotation changed, the security audits started happening more, and this has been going on for over a year. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but logs don’t prove intent, you know?\n\nThat’s the part people don’t get. In computer support, I’ve seen how documentation can be used later to make someone look like the problem. So yes, some of it is verifiable — the rotation, the audits, the logs — but what I’m saying is those things are being used to build a paper trail against me. And they act like I’m being unreasonable for noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that management's actions might not be intentionally targeted at you?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep asking me that, yes. I’m not trying to be stubborn. But I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that explains everything. But in computer support, logs don’t tell the whole story. I’ve seen situations where something looks routine on paper, and then later it gets used to say, “See, this person is the problem.” It’s like when my old supervisor years ago used to say documentation is only as neutral as the person writing it—well, not exactly those words, but that was the idea.\n\nSo, I mean, could it theoretically not be about me? Maybe. But after a year of it lining up the way it has, it doesn’t feel random. It feels directed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're being watched or monitored outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed, like, someone following me around or anything like that. Mostly what I pay attention to is the workplace stuff, because that’s where the logs and tickets and all that are.\n\nAlthough, you know, these days everything is connected anyway — phones, email, badge systems, all these accounts — but I don’t want to make it sound like I’m saying my family or neighbors are watching me. That’s not really what I’ve been trying to explain. My concern is more with management and what they’re documenting at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the belief that management is building a paper trail against you is something that might be happening only in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know why you’re asking that, but no, I don’t think it’s just in my mind. That’s the frustrating part. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s policy,” like that settles it. But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and policies can be applied in a way that looks neutral but isn’t. I remember at another job, not the same thing exactly, but there were these reports people acted like were objective, and later they became ammunition. So when I see the pattern now, I’m not inventing it. I’m noticing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you doubted your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve had moments where I tried to doubt it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit there and think, okay, maybe the ticket rotation really is just some new system they rolled out, maybe the security audits are just policy like they keep saying. They show me logs and reports and act like that should end the discussion.\n\nBut then I look at how it’s been going on for over a year, and how the same things keep getting documented around me, and it’s hard to believe it’s just routine. I’m in computer support, so I know how these records can look neutral while still being used to build a case. So the doubt doesn’t really last long. It keeps coming back to feeling like they’re making a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your interpretation of the situation at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.\n\nBut in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.\n\nAnd I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nBut I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. But I know how workplaces can set things up so it looks neutral when it really isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed someone was out to get you in a similar way before this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Similar before? Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.\n\nBut this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.\n\nSo… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another job where you felt management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. In computer support, everything gets documented—tickets, timestamps, escalations—and people act like that makes it neutral. But if you’ve been around long enough, you know documentation can be shaped.\n\nThere was one place years ago where they changed the metrics after a reorganization, and everyone suddenly had to justify why calls took longer, even though the systems were ancient—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWhat I’m trying to say is, no, I don’t think I had another job where I felt it this clearly. This current place is the one where, for over a year, I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Before that? Yeah, I mean… I didn’t think that before the schedule changed. It really started when they brought in the hybrid schedule and changed the after-hours ticket setup.\n\nThat was, what, about a year and two months ago now. Before then I wasn’t sitting around thinking management was building some case against me. I had normal work frustrations, like anyone—tickets piling up, people not reading instructions, that kind of thing—but this specific pattern started after those changes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But if you’ve been around long enough, you know documentation can be shaped.\n\nThere was one place years ago where they changed the metrics after a reorganization, and everyone suddenly had to justify why calls took longer, even though the systems were ancient—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWhat I’m trying to say is, no, I don’t think I had another job where I felt it this clearly. This current place is the one where, for over a year, I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Before that? Yeah, I mean… I didn’t think that before the schedule changed. It really started when they brought in the hybrid schedule and changed the after-hours ticket setup.\n\nThat was, what, about a year and two months ago now. Before then I wasn’t sitting around thinking management was building some case against me. I had normal work frustrations, like anyone—tickets piling up, people not reading instructions, that kind of thing—but this specific pattern started after those changes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think there's anything that could happen, or any evidence someone could show you, that would make you change your mind about management building a case against you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work, even though management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… if they showed me something real, maybe. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But that’s the problem—what they keep showing me are the same logs and ticket reports, and they say, “See, it’s just policy.” But policy doesn’t explain why the new rotation keeps putting me in certain spots, or why the security audits seem to land in a way that makes me look careless.

I worked in computer support a long time, so I know how clean a report can look when it’s generated. I used to help managers pull ticket histories, actually, years ago, and depending on what fields you include—sorry, I know that sounds like a tangent—but my point is logs don’t prove intent one way or the other.

So, could something change my mind? Maybe if there was some independent review that looked at the whole thing, not just the pieces management wants to show. But right now, no, the evidence they’ve shown me doesn’t change what I can see happening: the ticket rotation and the audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.

More than a month? Since it started, no, not really. I mean, before the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes, I wasn’t thinking that way at all. But once those changes came in—about a year and two months ago—it’s been there.

In computer support, I’ve seen how documentation can be used later to make someone look like the problem. I’m not trying to be stubborn. But in computer support, logs don’t tell the whole story. I’ve seen situations where something looks routine on paper, and then later it gets used to say, “See, this person is the problem.” It’s like when my old supervisor years ago used to say documentation is only as neutral as the person writing it—well, not exactly those words, but that was the idea.\n\nSo, I mean, could it theoretically not be about me? Maybe. But after a year of it lining up the way it has, it doesn’t feel random. It feels directed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're being watched or monitored outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed, like, someone following me around or anything like that. Mostly what I pay attention to is the workplace stuff, because that’s where the logs and tickets and all that are.\n\nAlthough, you know, these days everything is connected anyway — phones, email, badge systems, all these accounts — but I don’t want to make it sound like I’m saying my family or neighbors are watching me. That’s not really what I’ve been trying to explain. That’s the frustrating part. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s policy,” like that settles it. But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and policies can be applied in a way that looks neutral but isn’t. I remember at another job, not the same thing exactly, but there were these reports people acted like were objective, and later they became ammunition. So when I see the pattern now, I’m not inventing it. I’m noticing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you doubted your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve had moments where I tried to doubt it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit there and think, okay, maybe the ticket rotation really is just some new system they rolled out, maybe the security audits are just policy like they keep saying. They show me logs and reports and act like that should end the discussion.\n\nBut then I look at how it’s been going on for over a year, and how the same things keep getting documented around me, and it’s hard to believe it’s just routine. I’m in computer support, so I know how these records can look neutral while still being used to build a case. So the doubt doesn’t really last long. It keeps coming back to feeling like they’re making a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your interpretation of the situation at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.\n\nBut in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.\n\nAnd I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nBut I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. But I know how workplaces can set things up so it looks neutral when it really isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed someone was out to get you in a similar way before this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Similar before? Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.\n\nBut this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.\n\nSo… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another job where you felt management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. My concern is more with management and what they’re documenting at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the belief that management is building a paper trail against you is something that might be happening only in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know why you’re asking that, but no, I don’t think it’s just in my mind. That’s the frustrating part. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s policy,” like that settles it. But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and policies can be applied in a way that looks neutral but isn’t. I remember at another job, not the same thing exactly, but there were these reports people acted like were objective, and later they became ammunition. So when I see the pattern now, I’m not inventing it. I’m noticing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you doubted your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve had moments where I tried to doubt it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit there and think, okay, maybe the ticket rotation really is just some new system they rolled out, maybe the security audits are just policy like they keep saying. They show me logs and reports and act like that should end the discussion.\n\nBut then I look at how it’s been going on for over a year, and how the same things keep getting documented around me, and it’s hard to believe it’s just routine. I’m in computer support, so I know how these records can look neutral while still being used to build a case. So the doubt doesn’t really last long. It keeps coming back to feeling like they’re making a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your interpretation of the situation at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.\n\nBut in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.\n\nAnd I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nBut I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. But I know how workplaces can set things up so it looks neutral when it really isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed someone was out to get you in a similar way before this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Similar before? Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.\n\nBut this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.\n\nSo… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another job where you felt management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. In computer support, everything gets documented—tickets, timestamps, escalations—and people act like that makes it neutral. But if you’ve been around long enough, you know documentation can be shaped.\n\nThere was one place years ago where they changed the metrics after a reorganization, and everyone suddenly had to justify why calls took longer, even though the systems were ancient—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWhat I’m trying to say is, no, I don’t think I had another job where I felt it this clearly. This current place is the one where, for over a year, I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Before that? Yeah, I mean… I didn’t think that before the schedule changed. It really started when they brought in the hybrid schedule and changed the after-hours ticket setup.\n\nThat was, what, about a year and two months ago now. Before then I wasn’t sitting around thinking management was building some case against me. I had normal work frustrations, like anyone—tickets piling up, people not reading instructions, that kind of thing—but this specific pattern started after those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen, or any evidence someone could show you, that would make you change your mind about management building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… if they showed me something real, maybe. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But that’s the problem—what they keep showing me are the same logs and ticket reports, and they say, “See, it’s just policy.” But policy doesn’t explain why the new rotation keeps putting me in certain spots, or why the security audits seem to land in a way that makes me look careless.\n\nI worked in computer support a long time, so I know how clean a report can look when it’s generated. I used to help managers pull ticket histories, actually, years ago, and depending on what fields you include—sorry, I know that sounds like a tangent—but my point is logs don’t prove intent one way or the other.\n\nSo, could something change my mind? Maybe if there was some independent review that looked at the whole thing, not just the pieces management wants to show. But right now, no, the evidence they’ve shown me doesn’t change what I can see happening: the ticket rotation and the audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a month where you didn't think management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: More than a month? Since it started, no, not really. I mean, before the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes, I wasn’t thinking that way at all. But once those changes came in—about a year and two months ago—it’s been there.\n\nSome days I try not to focus on it, because you still have to do the work, answer the tickets, keep the users from panicking over printer drivers and passwords and all that. But not believing it? For a whole month? No. Not since those changes started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because I’ve been trying to get someone to actually listen to what’s been going on at work. It’s been over a year now with this new ticket rotation and the security audits, and I know management keeps saying it’s just policy, they even show me logs like that proves something, but it feels like they’re building a paper trail against me.\n\nI work in computer support, so I know how logs can be used, and I’ve seen enough “routine” procedures turn into blame later. Anyway, it’s not just a scheduling thing to me. I came because nobody there is taking it seriously.\n\nDoctor: Do you think management is intentionally trying to harm you or your career?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think there’s something intentional about it. I mean, they keep saying it’s just the new ticket rotation and the security audits, and they’ll pull up logs like that settles the whole matter, but logs only show what someone wants to track, you know? I’ve worked in computer support long enough to know how that stuff can be framed later.\n\nIt’s been going on for over a year, and it feels like they’re building a paper trail against me. Not “harm” like physically, but my career, yes. My reputation there. And once that starts, it’s hard to undo.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others outside of your workplace, such as friends or family, believe that management is intentionally trying to harm your career?\n\nPatient: My family mostly thinks I’m overreading it, I think. They haven’t seen the ticket rotation or the audit stuff the way I have. I try to explain that for over a year now this “policy” has been lining things up to make me look bad, like a paper trail, and management just keeps pointing to logs as if logs can’t be used that way.\n\nI mean, my brother doesn’t really understand computers beyond email, so when I say “logs,” he thinks it’s some neutral thing. But at work, in support, you know how these things can be framed. So no, I don’t think they fully believe me, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about management's intentions are based on facts that anyone could verify, or is it more of a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: I mean, there are facts there, yes. It’s not just a feeling. The ticket rotation changed, the security audits started happening more, and this has been going on for over a year. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” but logs don’t prove intent, you know?\n\nThat’s the part people don’t get. In computer support, I’ve seen how documentation can be used later to make someone look like the problem. So yes, some of it is verifiable — the rotation, the audits, the logs — but what I’m saying is those things are being used to build a paper trail against me. And they act like I’m being unreasonable for noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility that management's actions might not be intentionally targeted at you?\n\nPatient: I’ve considered it because people keep asking me that, yes. I’m not trying to be stubborn. But I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that explains everything. But in computer support, logs don’t tell the whole story. I’ve seen situations where something looks routine on paper, and then later it gets used to say, “See, this person is the problem.” It’s like when my old supervisor years ago used to say documentation is only as neutral as the person writing it—well, not exactly those words, but that was the idea.\n\nSo, I mean, could it theoretically not be about me? Maybe. But after a year of it lining up the way it has, it doesn’t feel random. It feels directed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're being watched or monitored outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed, like, someone following me around or anything like that. Mostly what I pay attention to is the workplace stuff, because that’s where the logs and tickets and all that are.\n\nAlthough, you know, these days everything is connected anyway — phones, email, badge systems, all these accounts — but I don’t want to make it sound like I’m saying my family or neighbors are watching me. That’s not really what I’ve been trying to explain. My concern is more with management and what they’re documenting at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the belief that management is building a paper trail against you is something that might be happening only in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know why you’re asking that, but no, I don’t think it’s just in my mind. That’s the frustrating part. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s policy,” like that settles it. But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and policies can be applied in a way that looks neutral but isn’t. I remember at another job, not the same thing exactly, but there were these reports people acted like were objective, and later they became ammunition. So when I see the pattern now, I’m not inventing it. I’m noticing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you doubted your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve had moments where I tried to doubt it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit there and think, okay, maybe the ticket rotation really is just some new system they rolled out, maybe the security audits are just policy like they keep saying. They show me logs and reports and act like that should end the discussion.\n\nBut then I look at how it’s been going on for over a year, and how the same things keep getting documented around me, and it’s hard to believe it’s just routine. I’m in computer support, so I know how these records can look neutral while still being used to build a case. So the doubt doesn’t really last long. It keeps coming back to feeling like they’re making a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your interpretation of the situation at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.\n\nBut in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.\n\nAnd I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nBut I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. But I know how workplaces can set things up so it looks neutral when it really isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed someone was out to get you in a similar way before this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Similar before? Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.\n\nBut this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.\n\nSo… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another job where you felt management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. In computer support, everything gets documented—tickets, timestamps, escalations—and people act like that makes it neutral. But if you’ve been around long enough, you know documentation can be shaped.\n\nThere was one place years ago where they changed the metrics after a reorganization, and everyone suddenly had to justify why calls took longer, even though the systems were ancient—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWhat I’m trying to say is, no, I don’t think I had another job where I felt it this clearly. This current place is the one where, for over a year, I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Before that? Yeah, I mean… I didn’t think that before the schedule changed. It really started when they brought in the hybrid schedule and changed the after-hours ticket setup.\n\nThat was, what, about a year and two months ago now. Before then I wasn’t sitting around thinking management was building some case against me. I had normal work frustrations, like anyone—tickets piling up, people not reading instructions, that kind of thing—but this specific pattern started after those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen, or any evidence someone could show you, that would make you change your mind about management building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… if they showed me something real, maybe. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But that’s the problem—what they keep showing me are the same logs and ticket reports, and they say, “See, it’s just policy.” But policy doesn’t explain why the new rotation keeps putting me in certain spots, or why the security audits seem to land in a way that makes me look careless.\n\nI worked in computer support a long time, so I know how clean a report can look when it’s generated. I used to help managers pull ticket histories, actually, years ago, and depending on what fields you include—sorry, I know that sounds like a tangent—but my point is logs don’t prove intent one way or the other.\n\nSo, could something change my mind? Maybe if there was some independent review that looked at the whole thing, not just the pieces management wants to show. But right now, no, the evidence they’ve shown me doesn’t change what I can see happening: the ticket rotation and the audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a month where you didn't think management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: More than a month? Since it started, no, not really. I mean, before the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes, I wasn’t thinking that way at all. But once those changes came in—about a year and two months ago—it’s been there.\n\nSome days I try not to focus on it, because you still have to do the work, answer the tickets, keep the users from panicking over printer drivers and passwords and all that. But not believing it? For a whole month? No. Not since those changes started.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: A whole day? I mean… maybe parts of a day where I’m busy enough not to think about it. Like if I’m buried in password resets or somebody’s laptop won’t connect to the VPN—those things eat up your attention.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I really believed management wasn’t doing it. For over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. They keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t explain the pattern I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could change your belief about management building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, if someone really looked at the whole thing fairly, maybe. Not just the logs management keeps pulling out and saying, “See, it’s policy.” I worked with reports before, and you can make them look very straightforward depending on what you include—ticket histories, timestamps, all that.\n\nBut right now, no, I don’t think anything they’ve shown me changes it. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. They keep acting like I’m overreacting, but I can see the pattern.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

---------- turn 18 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========

  {

Possible? I mean… I suppose anything is possible if someone actually looked at it straight. But just deciding to stop believing it, no. That’s not how it feels to me.

I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but logs don’t show you why certain things get assigned the way they do. I’ve worked with systems long enough to know you can make something look routine if you only show the right columns.

So… no, I don’t think I can just stop believing it because someone tells me to. Not when the pattern is still there.

It keeps coming back to feeling like they’re making a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your interpretation of the situation at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.\n\nBut in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another job where you felt management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. In computer support, everything gets documented—tickets, timestamps, escalations—and people act like that makes it neutral. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Before that? I’ve seen situations where something looks routine on paper, and then later it gets used to say, “See, this person is the problem.” It’s like when my old supervisor years ago used to say documentation is only as neutral as the person writing it—well, not exactly those words, but that was the idea.\n\nSo, I mean, could it theoretically not be about me? Maybe. But after a year of it lining up the way it has, it doesn’t feel random. It feels directed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're being watched or monitored outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t noticed, like, someone following me around or anything like that. Mostly what I pay attention to is the workplace stuff, because that’s where the logs and tickets and all that are.\n\nAlthough, you know, these days everything is connected anyway — phones, email, badge systems, all these accounts — but I don’t want to make it sound like I’m saying my family or neighbors are watching me. That’s not really what I’ve been trying to explain. My concern is more with management and what they’re documenting at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the belief that management is building a paper trail against you is something that might be happening only in your mind?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know why you’re asking that, but no, I don’t think it’s just in my mind. That’s the frustrating part. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work.\n\nManagement keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s policy,” like that settles it. But I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and policies can be applied in a way that looks neutral but isn’t. I remember at another job, not the same thing exactly, but there were these reports people acted like were objective, and later they became ammunition. So when I see the pattern now, I’m not inventing it. I’m noticing what’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a moment where you doubted your belief that management is intentionally building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I’ve had moments where I tried to doubt it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit there and think, okay, maybe the ticket rotation really is just some new system they rolled out, maybe the security audits are just policy like they keep saying. They show me logs and reports and act like that should end the discussion.\n\nBut then I look at how it’s been going on for over a year, and how the same things keep getting documented around me, and it’s hard to believe it’s just routine. I’m in computer support, so I know how these records can look neutral while still being used to build a case. So the doubt doesn’t really last long. It keeps coming back to feeling like they’re making a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your interpretation of the situation at work might be incorrect?\n\nPatient: I suppose anything is “possible,” sure. I mean, I worked in support long enough to know systems can be misunderstood—logs, permissions, ticket queues, all of that can look one way if you don’t have the full context.\n\nBut in this case… no, I don’t really think I’m reading it wrong. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.\n\nAnd I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nBut I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. But I know how workplaces can set things up so it looks neutral when it really isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed someone was out to get you in a similar way before this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Similar before? Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.\n\nBut this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.\n\nSo… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another job where you felt management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. In computer support, everything gets documented—tickets, timestamps, escalations—and people act like that makes it neutral. But if you’ve been around long enough, you know documentation can be shaped.\n\nThere was one place years ago where they changed the metrics after a reorganization, and everyone suddenly had to justify why calls took longer, even though the systems were ancient—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWhat I’m trying to say is, no, I don’t think I had another job where I felt it this clearly. This current place is the one where, for over a year, I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Before that? Yeah, I mean… I didn’t think that before the schedule changed. It really started when they brought in the hybrid schedule and changed the after-hours ticket setup.\n\nThat was, what, about a year and two months ago now. Before then I wasn’t sitting around thinking management was building some case against me. I had normal work frustrations, like anyone—tickets piling up, people not reading instructions, that kind of thing—but this specific pattern started after those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen, or any evidence someone could show you, that would make you change your mind about management building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… if they showed me something real, maybe. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I used to help managers pull ticket histories, actually, years ago, and depending on what fields you include—sorry, I know that sounds like a tangent—but my point is logs don’t prove intent one way or the other.\n\nSo, could something change my mind? Maybe if there was some independent review that looked at the whole thing, not just the pieces management wants to show. That’s why I’m here. For over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and these security audits aren’t just harmless policy. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “See, it’s just procedure,” but that doesn’t prove it isn’t being used to build a paper trail.\n\nAnd I’ve seen this kind of thing before—not exactly the same, but the way documentation starts piling up, and later everyone acts like it was obvious all along. So yes, technically I could be wrong, but that’s not what it looks like to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like someone or something was out to get you before this situation at work started?\n\nPatient: Before this specific thing at work? I mean… I wouldn’t say “out to get me” in some dramatic way. People use that phrase and it makes you sound paranoid, and that’s not what I’m trying to say.\n\nBut I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and I’ve seen how management can use procedures—ticket systems, audit logs, performance notes—to make something look objective. That’s what bothers me now. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me, even though they keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy.\n\nSo before this, no, not like this exactly. But I know how workplaces can set things up so it looks neutral when it really isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed someone was out to get you in a similar way before this situation at work?\n\nPatient: Similar before? Not really in the same way. I mean, I’ve had supervisors I didn’t trust, sure, but everybody has that at work. You learn after years in computer support that “policy” can mean a lot of things depending on who’s holding the paperwork.\n\nBut this situation is different. I’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, like that settles it, but logs can be used to tell whatever story they want if they’re collecting the right ones.\n\nSo… before this, no, I don’t think I believed someone was after me like this. This is specific to what’s happening at work now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another job where you felt management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: Another job? Not exactly… I mean, I’ve had managers who liked to hide behind procedures, sure. In computer support, everything gets documented—tickets, timestamps, escalations—and people act like that makes it neutral. But if you’ve been around long enough, you know documentation can be shaped.\n\nThere was one place years ago where they changed the metrics after a reorganization, and everyone suddenly had to justify why calls took longer, even though the systems were ancient—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWhat I’m trying to say is, no, I don’t think I had another job where I felt it this clearly. This current place is the one where, for over a year, I’ve been trying to get someone to see that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Management keeps pointing to logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t mean it isn’t being used for something else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Before that? Yeah, I mean… I didn’t think that before the schedule changed. It really started when they brought in the hybrid schedule and changed the after-hours ticket setup.\n\nThat was, what, about a year and two months ago now. Before then I wasn’t sitting around thinking management was building some case against me. I had normal work frustrations, like anyone—tickets piling up, people not reading instructions, that kind of thing—but this specific pattern started after those changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen, or any evidence someone could show you, that would make you change your mind about management building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I mean… if they showed me something real, maybe. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But that’s the problem—what they keep showing me are the same logs and ticket reports, and they say, “See, it’s just policy.” But policy doesn’t explain why the new rotation keeps putting me in certain spots, or why the security audits seem to land in a way that makes me look careless.\n\nI worked in computer support a long time, so I know how clean a report can look when it’s generated. I used to help managers pull ticket histories, actually, years ago, and depending on what fields you include—sorry, I know that sounds like a tangent—but my point is logs don’t prove intent one way or the other.\n\nSo, could something change my mind? Maybe if there was some independent review that looked at the whole thing, not just the pieces management wants to show. But right now, no, the evidence they’ve shown me doesn’t change what I can see happening: the ticket rotation and the audits are being used to build a paper trail against me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of more than a month where you didn't think management was building a case against you?\n\nPatient: More than a month? Since it started, no, not really. I mean, before the hybrid schedule and the after-hours ticket changes, I wasn’t thinking that way at all. But once those changes came in—about a year and two months ago—it’s been there.\n\nSome days I try not to focus on it, because you still have to do the work, answer the tickets, keep the users from panicking over printer drivers and passwords and all that. But not believing it? For a whole month? No. Not since those changes started.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a day where you didn't think management was out to get you?\n\nPatient: A whole day? I mean… maybe parts of a day where I’m busy enough not to think about it. Like if I’m buried in password resets or somebody’s laptop won’t connect to the VPN—those things eat up your attention.\n\nBut no, I don’t think there’s been a full day where I really believed management wasn’t doing it. They keep showing me logs and saying it’s just policy, but that doesn’t explain the pattern I’m seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could change your belief about management building a case against you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, if someone really looked at the whole thing fairly, maybe. Not just the logs management keeps pulling out and saying, “See, it’s policy.” I worked with reports before, and you can make them look very straightforward depending on what you include—ticket histories, timestamps, all that.\n\nBut right now, no, I don’t think anything they’ve shown me changes it. I’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to understand that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. They keep acting like I’m overreacting, but I can see the pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to stop believing that management is building a case against you?\n\nPatient: Possible? I mean… I suppose anything is possible if someone actually looked at it straight. But just deciding to stop believing it, no. That’s not how it feels to me.\n\nI’m here because for over a year I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and those security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. Management keeps showing me logs and saying, “It’s just policy,” but logs don’t show you why certain things get assigned the way they do. I’ve worked with systems long enough to know you can make something look routine if you only show the right columns.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I can just stop believing it because someone tells me to. Not when the pattern is still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that management is building a case against you could be a delusion?\n\nPatient: A delusion? I mean… that’s a strong word. I know that’s probably what people at work would like it to be called, because then nobody has to look at what’s actually happening.\n\nI’m here because for over a year now I’ve been trying to get someone to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and the security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me. Ever? I mean… I guess if there were a real explanation that accounted for all of it, maybe. But that’s the thing, they keep giving me these logs and saying, “See, it’s just policy,” like that settles it.

I’ve worked in computer support a long time, and systems don’t just tell the whole story by themselves. You can have a ticket rotation that looks neutral on paper, but depending on how it’s being used—well, I’ve seen plenty of reports where the important part is what isn’t shown.
